*Last updated: 2026-09-29 (`feat/queue-mid-turn` — user-requested: "new message/prompt sent in the running session in xdev has to add to queue and sent to session later like deepseek harness, and also have the button + hotkey to send now which the session will acknowledge and process that message too in the middle of session"). **The mid-turn submit queue shipped, the dsh/Claude-Code shape.** Until now a prompt typed while a turn was running was refused outright — `runTurn`'s single `running` claim printed "a turn is already running — Esc cancels it" and the words were gone, so the only way to talk to a working model was to kill the turn. The agent half already existed (`Agent.Steer` + the loop's steering drain, `internal/agent/loop.go`); what was missing was the half a person can see, and that is what this adds:

1. **Enter while working = queue + steer into the SAME run.** `internal/tui/queue.go` owns the pending list; `tui.App.SetQueueHandlers`'s `onQueue` seam resolves to `curAgent.Steer(text)` in `cmd/xdev/tui.go`, so the message is injected as a user message at the run's next step boundary and persisted by the loop. A host that declines (a guest room with no local run) returns false and the row is withdrawn — a row must never promise a delivery that will not happen.
2. **A visible, append-only, oldest-first list above the composer** — ordinal, kind mark (`❯` / `!` shell / `/` command), flattened one-line text, a `[send now]` button, and `+N more queued` when it overflows the five-row window. Order is never rewritten: a queue that re-sorts is a queue nobody trusts. A click on a row body is take-back — the text returns to the composer, newlines intact.
3. **Retirement is the agent's, not the UI's.** New `agent.Agent.SteeringDelivered func([]string)` fires from both steering drains (step boundary and post-final-tool-call) AFTER the message is persisted, and the TUI's `RetireDelivered` drops exactly those rows. A row used to have no way to know it had been sent; matching is by value oldest-first, so the same sentence typed twice retires twice, and a foreign steer (extension, mailbox) is a no-op.
4. **`[send now]` + F6 = interrupt, acknowledge, run.** A new remappable keymap action (`send-now`, listed in `/hotkeys`) and the per-row button both call the host's immediate-delivery seam: abort the live turn, print `· interrupted — delivering now`, wait (bounded, 2 s) for the run to release its turn claim, then persist the message and start a fresh turn. Interrupting rather than injecting is the honest reading of "now" — the in-flight provider request cannot be pre-empted. With an empty queue the chord says so instead of doing nothing silently.
5. **A row that outlives its run is flushed, not stranded.** `flushPendingQueue` is deferred first in the turn goroutine (LIFO: it runs last, after `running.Store(false)`) and turns the oldest undelivered row into the next turn, one row per run — a prompt typed as a run ended was never seen by the model, and the honest outcome is to run it, not to leave a row claiming otherwise.

Attachments are deliberately NOT queued: a queued image has no honest delivery story (the pixels are not in the steer channel), so it keeps the old path and comes back to the composer with its reason. The residual gap against CC's matrix (#157's remaining parts) is the take-back-ALL / Esc-flush gesture and the per-kind command policy (`/status`-class commands running immediately); those are untouched here.

*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`feat/status-pills` follow-up — user-reported on the live row: "the icon is overlap the number"). **The gauge icon was a two-cell glyph, so it painted over the first digit of the time beside it.** `theme.HUDIcon(HUDIconGauge)` returned U+23F1 STOPWATCH, which runewwidth calls one cell but which most terminals paint TWO (East Asian Ambiguous): the status row writes the icon into one cell, the terminal's own second column lands on the first character of the number, and `⏱9s` reads as the clock sitting on the 9. Two fixes, because either alone is half the answer: **the shipped gauge is U+23F2 STOPWATCH** (Unambiguous, one cell in every terminal that has the glyph), and **`HUDIcon` cuts its result to a single character**, so a theme whose `symbols.overrides["hud.gauge"]` holds a two-cell glyph gets the same collision back — one character of icon, or nothing. ▤ and ✳ were already one cell everywhere. Pinned by `TestHUDIconIsAlwaysOneCharacter` (one character for all three defaults, the gauge is not U+23F1, a two-character override is cut) and re-verified on a real pty: the row replays cell by cell as `⏲13s · 1t·1g │ ▤9.4k · 99%`, the cell after the icon is the digit `1`, and every pill click and exit still holds.

Both are kept in paint order — the pill popup first (anchored to the status row), then the selection highlight, then the toasts last over everything, because a corner toast is the one thing that must never end up under a highlight or a picker frame. `docs/PRD.md`: four new footers, appended in date order.

*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`feat/sidebar-toggle` — user-requested: "add the toggle sidebar to show/hide in xdev"). **The dock had a policy but no switch.** `Alt+S` walks the three states (`show` → `hide` → `auto`) and `/settings sidebarMode` is the same three, so a human who wanted the sidebar *gone* — the one thing both doors are bad at, since the cycle's next stop after `hide` is `auto` and the panel is back at 120+ columns — had no single move that hides it. `/sidebar [show|hide|auto]` (alias `/dock`) is that move: bare toggles what is actually painted, so an auto-closed panel is opened rather than "hidden" a second time, and it writes the same `sidebarMode` key through the same seam, so the command, the chord, the settings row and the global config cannot disagree. Verified: `go build ./...` and `go vet ./...` clean; `go test ./internal/tui/` green, including `TestSidebarCommandTogglesWhatIsOnScreen` (the toggle answers the screen, not the policy, persists both directions, and refuses an unknown word); the built binary driven through tmux at 160×40 — `/sidebar` → hide → show → `auto` each repainted the panel and the confirm line, `/sidebar sideways` printed the usage error, nothing reached the model.*
*Last updated 2026-09-30 (`fix/status-pill-click` — user-requested, three symptoms on the live status row: "remove icon in the bottom status bar", "make the tooltip when user click to metrics in status bard background color to black", "just 1 click to show, 1 click again to hide, no need to click and hold"). **Three changes, all in `internal/tui/statuspill.go`; the shipped layout is unchanged (`command │ time │ tokens`), so `defaultStatusSegments` and the keep-ranks did not move.**

**(1) The two pills lost their icons.** `pillLabel` no longer prefixes `theme.HUDIcon(HUDIconGauge)` (⏲) or `theme.HUDIcon(HUDIconDatabase)` (▤), so the row reads `1s · 1t·1g · 1516.3 t/s │ 50.1k · 97%` and gives the two cells back to the working-directory path. dsh draws an icon per pill because it draws a row of buttons in a browser; a terminal row is a fixed-cell grid whose every column is contested, and the icon only said which family a figure belonged to — a distinction the value's own unit already makes. The theme vocabulary is untouched: `theme.HUDIcon` still exists and the opt-in segments that wear a glyph (`rate`, `ttft`, `toolcalls`, `split`) keep theirs, so `symbols.overrides["hud.*"]` is still a live knob for a session that configured one of those.

**(2) The panel's field is the terminal's own background (SGR 49), not `theme.BgBase`.** The "black" the user asked for is the one call `#495` made for the sidebar: `BgBase` (`#141414`) is not black but a grey band, and it was painted under text cells that resolve to the terminal's black anyway — the same striping `#466` built out of the diff popup. On the dark terminal this reads black; on the built-in light theme it stays light rather than stranding dark label/value inks on a black box.

**(3) One click shows, one click hides — no press-and-hold.** The root cause was a one-word mistake, not a gesture: `handleStatusPopupMouse` dismissed the panel on ANY mouse report outside it, and the release of the very gesture that opened it lands on the pill's own cell — the status row, one row BELOW the panel that just went up. So the button-up closed it again and the popup read as a hold-to-peek. The dismiss is now gated on `press`, so only a new click elsewhere closes it (clicking the other pill still switches panels, click-outside still dismisses, Esc still closes).

Tests: `internal/tui/statuspill_test.go` gains `TestPillStaysUpUntilTheNextClick` (the whole gesture driven through `handleKey` — press, paint, release, paint — must leave the panel up, and a second click must take it down; it fails on `origin/main` with "the panel closed on the button-up") and `TestPillPopupIsTheTerminalBackground` (an interior cell and a label cell must both resolve to `ColorDefault` while the built-in theme still names `bg_base`; fails on `origin/main` with "popup cell (116,21) ' ' has background #141414"). `TestPillsReadDshsOwnLabels` now asserts the row wears neither ⏲ nor ▤, and the icon-needle rows in `chrome_test.go` / `usage_test.go` / `sidebar_fullheight_test.go` / `cmd/xdev/tui_metric_wire_test.go` match the bare figures; `TestCacheSegmentCarriesNoSecondTotal` loses its "the database icon is drawn exactly once" claim, which was a claim about the pills, not about the `cache` segment it tests.

`go build ./...`, `go vet ./internal/tui/`, `go test ./internal/tui/ ./cmd/xdev/` green (the two `cmd/xdev` failures, `TestConnectPickerItems` and `TestSkillPromptBlockEmptyWithoutSkills`, fail identically on `origin/main` — they read this machine's `~/.xdev` and `~/.xdev/agent/skills`, not the repo). **Verified live, not only in unit tests:** the built binary on a pty at 160×40 with `env -u NO_COLOR`, a stub gateway streaming a real turn so both pills carry a session, and SGR 1006 reports fed as the bytes a terminal sends. The status row paints `1s · 1t·1g · 1516.3 t/s │ 50.1k · 97%`, one click on the time pill brings up `Session statistics` (`turns / steps 1 / 1`, `LLM time`, `decode speed`, `active time`) and it is still up after the release, a second click takes it down, one click on the token pill shows `Token usage`, and a click on the transcript dismisses. Every cell of the panel's interior resolves to SGR 49, i.e. the terminal's own background.

*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`feat/bash-live-output`). **A running `bash` was a spinner and nothing else — its output appeared all at once, after the command finished.** `runShell`'s copier goroutines wrote straight into the head+tail `OutputSink`s and nothing else was told, so the transcript had nothing to paint until `Execute` returned; a three-second build looked like a hang and a ten-second test looked like one twice. **A live-output seam, four pieces, no change to what the model sees.** `tool.OutputObserver` ([internal/tool/liveoutput.go](https://github.com/FreePeak/xdev/blob/main/internal/tool/liveoutput.go)) rides the CALL CONTEXT (`WithOutputObserver`/`OutputObserverOf`), not a tool field, because one `BashTool` instance serves every concurrent call — so the call id has to travel with the bytes. `switchWriter` grew a `tap` and calls it AFTER the sink write, under its own lock: the sink gets the bytes first, so a slow observer can delay the transcript but never the child whose pipe would back up. `Agent.OnOutput func(callID, name, chunk string)` is the agent-level seam; `runOneTool` wraps it per call so two concurrent `bash` calls' output can be routed — the same mis-pairing `FinishTool` is matched by call id to avoid. Only the TUI wires it (`app.AppendToolOutput`); print mode, RPC and every tool that does not stream (read, grep, MCP) keep the untouched context. **The TUI side is one block, opened by the first chunk.** `App.AppendToolOutput(callID, name, chunk)` finds or opens a `Block{Kind: KindToolDone, Live: true}` **spliced under its own call row** — never appended at the tail, which for a concurrent batch is whatever call started last — bounded at 64 KiB of tail (`liveTailBytes`), and stamped `liveSeq` at most once per 100 ms (`livePaint`, the ~10 fps the user picked) so a tool flooding chunks costs the frames a reader can see, not one frame per chunk. The stamp is on `blockKey` as well as `Block`, because length alone does not move for a progress bar rewriting the same window of bytes. A live box renders its newest `liveRows` (= `toolRecentHead`) rows and **no footer** — a running command has no exit code it has earned, and no Ctrl+O over output that is still arriving. `FinishTool` REPLACES the box with the settled result (one command, one box: two is the transcript lying about what ran). A tool that streams nothing opens no box, and a chunk for a call that never ran or has settled is dropped, so `!bash` / composer shell mode and every silent tool are byte-for-byte unchanged.  Mutation-verified: deleting `live: b.liveSeq` from `blockKey` fails `TestLiveBoxRepaintsOnceTheTailCapFreezesItsLength` (a flooding command's box freezes once the tail cap holds its length steady) and `TestLiveBoxStampCoalescesButAlwaysMoves`; deleting the wire (`Agent.OnOutput`) fails `TestLiveOutputReachesTheScreenWhileTheCallRuns`.  **Driven by hand against the real binary, in a pty, A/B against the base commit** (worktree `live-base-probe` at `6d5f0c0`, both built from source, one scripted `openai-completions` server on `127.0.0.1` driving a real `bash`): the tool call runs `for i in 1..8; do echo tick-$i; sleep 0.4; done` (~3.3s). **With live output each line reaches the terminal as it is produced — 0.2s, 0.6s, 1.0s, 1.5s, 1.9s, 2.3s, 2.7s, 3.1s, ~0.4s apart, matching the command exactly. On the base build all eight appear at 3.5s, in one burst, when the command exits** — which is the spinner-and-nothing defect this change exists to remove, measured rather than argued. The settled result is identical on both (the tail is present either way), so nothing the model sees moved. The 4,000-line flood case (`i=0; while [ $i -lt 4000 ]`) also completes and settles with the tail intact on both builds. Pinned by `TestBashOutputObserverSeesStreamWhileRunning` (a chunk exists BEFORE `Execute` returns), `TestOnOutputCarriesTheCallIdentity` (concurrent calls each stamped with their own id), `TestLiveBoxSitsUnderItsOwnCallRow` / `TestLiveBoxSpliceInvalidatesTheRowIndex` (splice position and the index-aligned row index), `TestLiveBoxStampCoalescesButAlwaysMoves` (coalescing, and a same-length rewrite still repaints), `TestLiveBoxRepaintsOnceTheTailCapFreezesItsLength` (the cap itself manufactures the same-length rewrite, so the box's own bound is where a length-only stamp would freeze), `TestLiveBoxIsBoundedAndKeepsTheTail`, `TestFinishToolReplacesTheLiveBox`, `TestFinishToolKeepsTheResultUnderItsCallRow` (the settled result rewrites the box's slot rather than being appended above a later call's row), and — at the host boundary, on SCREEN rather than in app state — `TestWiredTUIHostPaintsARunningBashCall` and `TestLiveOutputReachesTheScreenWhileTheCallRuns` (a real `bash`, the real `tuiHooks`, the real paint loop, read off a simulation screen).


*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`docs/prd-syntax-highlight`). **A PRD line described a feature that does not exist.** The scrollback block-styling bullet promised "fenced code with syntax highlighting via bundled tmTheme mapping" — `internal/tui/markdown.go` paints every fenced line as one run in the body ink as a single `md_text`-coloured run on the `md_code_bg` band (`md_code` is the INLINE-code ink, not the fenced one), no `.tmTheme` exists anywhere in the repo, and `grep -rn "theme.Syntax" internal/ --include=*.go | grep -v ^internal/theme/` returns nothing, so all 9 `syntax_*` tokens are settable and unread. The line now states what renders and names the theme tokens as unconsumed; the feature itself moves to [#501](https://github.com/FreePeak/xdev/issues/501) rather than being quietly deleted, since the tokens are the intended surface. No code changed.*

*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`feat/code-highlight`, closing [#501](https://github.com/FreePeak/xdev/issues/501)). **The 9 `syntax_*` tokens finally have a consumer.** A fenced block was one run in the body ink, so comment, string and keyword painted identically; `internal/tui/highlight.go` is a hand-written per-line scanner that maps each token onto one of the 9 existing slots, with the launch palettes pinning all 9 from the same TokyoNight Night accents the rest of groknight uses. No tree-sitter and no tmTheme: the constraint is `go.mod`'s CGO-free guarantee (§3.6), the same one that keeps `ast_grep` shelling out to the `ast-grep` binary. The honest limits, all pinned by tests: it lexes ONE line at a time, so a block comment spanning lines is coloured in pieces; a mis-token paints the wrong colour and never drops or reorders a byte (`runsString(runs)` is byte-identical to the source line, which is what the selection/copy path reads). `NO_COLOR` is gated in the RENDERER rather than left to tcell — tcell drops colour at emission, so an ink chosen here would appear in every cell dump and screenshot while the terminal showed a single colour. `internal/tui/markdown.go` is the only render call site that changed: the fence line now records the info string instead of toggling a bare boolean.*

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/mermaid-render` — the parser refused diagrams agents actually write: edge chains, inline edge labels, bare shaped nodes, commas in labels, subgraphs, sequence activation and control blocks, and `stateDiagram-v2` all fell back to the source). Three common cases render now: **an edge chain** `A --> B --> C` is two edges off three nodes — the old walker consumed the first arrow and then read the second arrow as the target; **an inline edge label** `D -- resume() --> B` and its dotted sibling `T -. isolated .-> B`, where the closing arrow is as far away as the label is long, so the arrow scan is bracket- and quote-aware rather than a token list; **a bare node** `IDX[("Index")]`, which has no edge at all and used to be a whole-block fallback. A comma inside a bracketed label is the label (`A[docs, notes, specs]`), and a `subgraph` is a frame around its members rather than a refusal. **On a sequence diagram** the `+` of `A->>+B` and the `-` of `B-->>-A` are activation markers, not part of a name, and a control block (`loop`, `alt`, `else`, `opt`, `par`, `critical`, `rect`) is a labelled row whose messages still draw. **`stateDiagram-v2` is a flowchart in different punctuation** — a transition is `A --> B: event` not `A -->|event| B`, `[*]` is the marker, `state "Long" as id` names a state — so it is translated to the flow spelling and handed to the same reader, one graph, one layout, one drawer. Unreadable source still falls back whole; the fallback is the safety property and it did not move. **An answer that ends in a ```mermaid fence was a code band** — the reader had the source and no picture, which is the one thing a diagram is for. `internal/tui/mermaid.go` now parses a fence and draws it, and `renderMarkdown` gives the `mermaid` fence tag first refusal on its body: `fenceLang` reads the info string, `fenceBody` gathers the block, `mermaidLines` draws it, and **every non-result is a nil that puts the code band back exactly as it was** — an unsupported type (class/state/ER/gantt/xychart), a source the parser cannot read, a subgraph, or a diagram that cannot fit the terminal. The fallback is the whole promise: a wrong diagram is worse than source, because a reader cannot tell a parser dropped a line.

Two types render, and the split is the one that survives a terminal. **A sequence diagram is a lifeline grid** (`drawSequence`): one boxed head per participant, a dotted lifeline per column, and per message a wire row (a solid run with `▶` for a call, a dotted run with `◀` for a reply) with its label on the row below, so an arrow never shares a row with the text that would cross it. A self-message is a two-row hook; a `Note over A,B` is a `║ … ║` band. A participant that never appears in a message is dropped rather than given a truncated column, and a diagram whose participant columns do not fit becomes a **call log** (`drawSequenceLog`) — every message still its own rows, endpoints spelled out, labels wrapped as prose. Geometry is lost, content is not. **A flowchart is a graph, so it gets a layout** (`drawFlow` → `drawFlowAt`): longest-path ranks, boxes 3 rows tall, TD ranks stacked with a label row, an elbow row and a head row between them, edges drawn as straight elbows with a label on the row above the horizontal run, and a last-resort **outline** listing (node, then its edges) when no layout fits.

The drawing happens on a rune canvas with a parallel role canvas (`grid`), which is what lets the roles reach the paint: box/wire `md_muted`, arrowhead `md_code`, node label bold `md_code`, body `text_secondary`, note italic `gray_dim` — and the rows are cut into runs **by rune, not byte**, because a byte-indexed split slices a multi-byte box glyph in half and the transcript then paints a replacement character where the wire should be. `mmGlyphs` swaps the whole vocabulary for the `ascii` symbol preset. Everything is bounded: ≤ 240 rows, ≤ 80 nodes, ≤ 200 messages, ≤ 32 cells per label.

Config: **`renderMermaid` (default on, `*bool` for `showThinking`'s reason — the zero-skip merge cannot tell unset from off)**, on the repo-safe key list, in `xdev config list`, in `fallbackValue`, one toggle row in the Alt+, settings panel (applied live through `applySettingImmediate`), and `/settings renderMermaid [on|off]` persisting through the new `SettingsOps.SetMermaid` seam. It is display-only: `SetRenderMermaid` drops the render cache and the new `blockKey.mermaid` bit re-stamps every block, so a flip repaints the transcript in both directions and the source text is never touched.

Tests: `internal/tui/mermaid_test.go` — the call-chain fixture (four participants, a storage hop, a note, a reply) drawn in full; arrow direction per message; the narrow collapse keeping all six messages; self-message hook; every flow edge drawing a head; fan-out spreading into three columns; a cycle terminating the ranking; unsupported types and unreadable sources falling back; the edge-chain case drawing every hop; both edge-label spellings and the dotted one reaching the render; a bare shaped node; a comma in a label not inventing a node; a subgraph rendering; activation markers; the three control blocks; `stateDiagram-v2` with its event labels and its `state ... as` naming; every rendered row fitting at w = 10…200; the ascii preset emitting no non-ASCII; three distinct inks reaching the paint; the fence tag deciding in Markdown; the fallback banding its rows; the live two-way toggle; and the slash setting persisting. `internal/config/settings_test.go` gains `TestRenderMermaidDefaultsOnAndHonoursExplicitFalse` (default on, project layer wins, explicit `false` survives the merge, `config set` reaches the key). `go build ./...`, `go vet ./internal/tui/ ./internal/config/ ./cmd/xdev/`, `go test ./internal/tui/ ./internal/config/` green.

*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`fix/think-box-one-row` — user-reported: "change thinking box to 1 rows display only", then "when click the box increase the height like current one which focused and scrollable, and click to that box again or click outside to lost focus, the thinking box back to 1 row again"): **a reasoning box was 12 rows before anyone asked for it.** Every thought painted a fixed 12-row window plus a hidden-row notice, so a turn with four thoughts spent 50+ rows of scrollback on frame and the answer arrived below the fold. The box now has one collapsed row — the newest thought, tail-anchored, no notice, since a notice would cost the only row there is — and a click grows it to the window that already existed: bold border, `… N rows hidden (Ctrl+O to expand)`, wheel-scrolled. The focus is the one that was already there (`App.thinkFocus`, set by the press arm in `selection.go`, drawn bold), so no new state: height is read off it, and a second click on the box or a click anywhere else puts it back to one row with `ThinkOff` intact, so a re-opened box is where it was left. `Ctrl+O` (Block.Expanded) is the one height focus does not own. `thinkWindow`/`thinkMaxOff` now take the height (`thinkWindow(n, off, rows)`) so the render and the wheel cannot disagree about where the box stops.

*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`fix/stream-end-retry` — user-reported: "xdev not retry with this error: error: agent: stream: openai-completions: stream ended without finish_reason"). **The error was already classified transient; the ladder just never saw it in one shape.** `ai.Classify` reads the adapter's own sentinel through `prematureCloseRe` (`internal/ai/errors.go`), and `recovery_test.go` already pins the pre-content case — a stream that dies before any content is retried and the run resumes. What escaped was the other shape, and it carries the *same* message: a stream cut **after a tool call started, with no text behind it**. `oneTurn` had set `emitted = true` on the call's `EventToolcallStart`, so the ladder took the post-content branch — but there was no partial to retain (the adapters emit `EventToolcallEnd` only on their terminal path, so a call whose stream dies is never persisted) and `canRetain` was false, so `return nil, history, err` surfaced the error raw. The session ended on a failure the ladder was built to survive.

**The change:** the turn now reports what it left behind. `turnError.orphanToolCalls` counts the unpaired calls, and the transient branch treats that one case as replayable rather than un-continuable: `if turnContentEmitted(err) && orphans == 0` gates the retain-and-continue arm, so a cut with no partial falls through to the pre-content ladder (backoff → replay, then the same failover/escalation a cold death gets). The orphan itself is *not* persisted — a whole-turn re-send regenerates the call from the same history, which is exactly what a retained partial could not do, and a re-sent unpaired call is the shape providers reject (the reason tool calls are excluded from partials in the first place).

Deliberately narrow: only a cut that rendered **nothing** is replayed this way. Once text is on screen, replaying would double-emit it, so those turns keep the retain-and-continue path and its `maxPostContentContinuations` ceiling untouched.

Tests: `internal/agent/recovery_test.go` (`TestStreamEndedMidToolCallReplaysTheTurn` — the exact adapter sentinel, delivered after a `toolcall_start` with a half-streamed argument, must replay the turn and answer, with no request carrying the orphaned call; fails on stock `main` with `a cut mid-tool-call must be replayed, not surfaced: agent: stream: openai-completions: stream ended without finish_reason`, which is the bug in one assertion). Existing pins unchanged and green: `TestStreamEndedWithoutFinishReasonRetriesAndResumes` (the pre-content half), `TestRetryPostContentSecondFailureSurfaces` and `TestPostContentContinuationIsBoundedUnderInfiniteRetry` (the retained-partial budget), `TestMalformedStreamRetriesAndResumes` / `TestMalformedStreamAfterContentRetainsAndContinues` (the sibling clean-EOF shapes). Verified: `go build ./...`, `go vet ./internal/agent/`, `gofmt` clean, `go test ./internal/agent/ ./internal/ai/...` green.

Note: this entry covers the mid-tool-call shape only. The pre-content and post-text shapes of this error already retried before this change. A report of "not retrying" that carried no `· stream error — retrying (xN)` line above it and no `recovery:` line in the file is a different failure — a subagent's own `Run` (`internal/agent/subagent.go`), or the `maxPostContentContinuations = 3` budget draining after consecutive mid-message cuts, which surfaces by design — and is not addressed here.

*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (feat/session-tabs): the TUI holds a live session set (`tabset`, up to 6 open stores). `/new` and `/resume` park the previous session instead of closing it — its turn keeps running, and `tuiHooks` only paints while that session is focused (parked output raises an unread badge). Alt+]/Alt+[ cycle the set; Alt+Shift+]/[ jump to unread; the status row's `sessions` segment shows `N tabs ·busy ✦unread`. Esc aborts only the foreground session. This is the opencode session-tabs model (route.navigate + per-session-id RunCoordinator) ported to xdev's single-process TUI: no server, no tab strip paint yet — the badge and the cycle chords are the whole UI. Issue #131 (background-session fleet / `--bg`) stays separate: that is multi-process attach, this is multi-session in one TUI.*
*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (feat/session-bg, issue #131 slice 1): `xdev --bg "prompt"` detaches a print-mode worker (new session via setsid) so the job survives terminal kill. Status + log live under `~/.xdev/agent/bg/<id>/`; `xdev bg list|logs|stop|rm` manage them. Hang caps: --bg defaults `--max-time` to 2h when unset; stream first-progress/idle watchdogs and `--max-turns` still apply. No supervisor daemon and no attach TUI yet — those are the #131 residual.*
*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (`fix/stuck-quit`). **A stuck xdev was un-exitable: Ctrl+C, Ctrl+D, nothing.** The stall watchdog wrote a dump and left the user in a raw-mode alt screen with a live process, because the quit chord is applied by the very loop that had wedged — a loop blocked 17m inside tcell's synchronous tty write reads no key at all (session 00b1c5a0; the worst of 13 dumps that day). Now the poll goroutine serves the `quit` chord whenever the watchdog has condemned the loop, and a loop still wedged ten minutes after its first dump restores the terminal and exits non-zero instead of living forever. `go build ./...`, `go vet ./internal/tui/ ./cmd/xdev/`, `go test ./internal/tui/ -race` green for the new tests (the two `cmd/xdev` failures and the `TestBeatDoneNamesASlowIteration` / `TestOnlyTheReadError` races fail identically on `origin/main`).*
*Last updated: 2026-09-30 (feat/tui-exit-detach): quitting the TUI with a turn in flight **detaches by default** (opencode parity — the TUI is a client, the run outlives it). Setting `tui.exitDetach` (default on; `xdev config set tui.exitDetach false` or Alt+, "Detach on quit") restores kill-on-exit. Detach cancels the in-process turn and spawns a `--bg` print worker that `--resume`s the same session; hang caps are the bg defaults (2h max-time + stream watchdogs). Esc stays cancel-only.*
*Last updated: 2026-10-01 (`fix/bare-thinking-label` — user-reported: "the sidebar now showing the model like the divider too"). **The reasoning readout said "thinking" twice over.** `App.thinkingLabel` prefixed the rung with the word, so the divider painted `onegw/…-free · thinking high` and the SESSION footer a bare `thinking high` row — beside a model name, so a rung like `high` on its own line read as a second name. The seam is now `App.thinkingLevel`: the bare rung, nothing else, unwired still empty. The divider is `model · <level>`, and the footer pairs the model and the level in ONE row (`onegw/…-free · high`) — the two are one request, and a row that pairs them cannot be misread the way a bare `high` under a path and a branch could. `/thinking` keeps its name: the word is the command, the readout is the answer.*

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (feat/tabs-picker): **the open sessions had no list.** The tab set could be cycled (Alt+]/Alt+[) and counted on the status row (`2 tabs`), but nothing showed WHO was open, so switching meant counting. `/tabs` opens the generic picker over the snapshot `SetTabs` already publishes — one row per open session (title, short id, a `·running` badge), the current one marked, type-to-filter, Enter switches through the new `tabset.focus` and the same `focusTab` rebuild `/resume` takes, so a parked turn keeps running. No second source of truth: the rows are the status row's own data. `tabset.focus` is a one-liner over `indexOfLocked` + `activate` (the file already held both), and `App.TabsPicker` is the standard picker — which is why the diff is ~200 lines of wiring and three tests, not a new view. ponytail: the picker has no pin/delete/filter-by-project extras; those live on `/resume`, where the sessions are files on disk.
*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (feat/leader-keys-tabbar — user-requested "use the keybindings from opencode and config for my xdev" + "add the session tab management like opencode too"): **the keymap gained a leader prefix, and the tab set became visible.** opencode's `tui.json` binds most of its session keys as `<leader>` pairs (Ctrl+X then w/n/l/q) — a shape xdev's KeyMap could not express, because it resolved exactly one key event per chord. `KeyMap` now holds `leader` + an armed `pending` prefix with a 2s deadline (opencode's `leader_timeout`), and `resolve` probes `pending+" "+chord` before the table; a follow-up that completes no pair falls through to its own binding, so Ctrl+X never becomes a dead key. `LoadKeyMap` accepts opencode's exact spelling: `<leader>w` and comma-joined `"<leader>w,alt+w"` expand at load, and `ctrl+d`/`alt+w` fold to `C-d`/`A-w` — a config pasted from an opencode `tui.json` resolves unedited. New actions: `session.new` (Ctrl+X n), `session.list` (Ctrl+X l → the `/tabs` picker), `session.delete` (Ctrl+X w / Ctrl+X d / Alt+W / **Ctrl+D**). Ctrl+D was `quit` and is now `session.delete`: it closes the current tab and falls back to quit on the LAST one, so one chord still ends the app and a chord that looks broken never appears. `<leader>q` and Ctrl+C quit. The tab strip (`tabstrip.go`) is row 1 under the top bar, drawn only with 2+ sessions open: ✦ on a running one, underline on the current, a `×` per cell. Click a label to focus, the `×` to close — both route through `SetTabClose`/`SetTabPick`, so the mouse and the chords cannot diverge; `cmd/xdev`'s `closeTabByID` owns parking, aborting a live turn and focusing the neighbour, and the session FILE survives (that is still `/drop`). `transcriptTop()` accounts for the strip, so the transcript cannot start underneath it. `Chord` now reads `Chords` rather than the sorted table, so a status-row hint advertises the portable chord. Tests: `leaderkeys_test.go` pins the pairs, the fall-through, the timeout, opencode-spelled config, C-d-quits-on-last-tab, and the label-vs-× hit split. Installed for this user at `~/.xdev/agent/keybindings.yml`.

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/leader-pair-dead` — found while verifying the leader chords end to end): **every pair was dead in the real UI.** `App.handleKey` resolved the key TWICE on one event — once at the top into `action`, and again at the editor-adjacent switch. The second `Resolve` saw the prefix the first had just armed, consumed it, and looked up the bare follow-up key: Ctrl+X then L resolved as `l` and no pair fired. The map-level tests all passed because they called `KeyMap.resolve` directly and never went through `handleKey` — the engine was right and the wiring was wrong. The editor-adjacent switch now reuses the already-resolved `action`. The wedged-loop probe (`app.go` — the C-c escape hatch for a stuck UI thread) is the only other `Resolve` call site and is deliberately left alone: it answers solely for `quit`, which has no prefix form, and it runs on the feed goroutine while the loop is stuck. `internal/tui/leaderhandle_test.go` drives `App.handleKey` — the same entry `Run`'s loop uses — for C-x l, C-x w, expiry, fall-through, and the prefix typing nothing. Verified: three of the five FAIL on the parent commit `8d12d4d` and pass on `b61535a`.
*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/upstream-400-retry` — user-reported: "xdev not retry with this error: `agent: stream start: openai-completions: HTTP 400 {"error":{"code":"400","message":"Upstream request failed: [invalid_request_error] invalid request","type":"invalid_request_error"}}`"). **A gateway-relayed upstream 400 ended the run.** `ai.upstreamRefusalRe` only matched the `Error from provider (<name>): Upstream request …` phrasing, so onegw/OpenCode's bare `Upstream request failed: [invalid_request_error] invalid request` — which names no provider and still relays an upstream verdict — read as a shape rejection (`ClassBadRequest`). That branch is capped at `maxEscalationRounds` (two quick retries) and ignores `retry.infinite` by design, so a turn that a history rebuild could not repair ended the session. The regex now matches the upstream verdict itself (`upstream request failed|could not be processed|response was not valid json`), independent of the "from provider" wrapper, so the body classifies `ClassTransient` and takes the full ladder: backoff in place, failover to the next chain target, then escalation rounds. A 400 that names no upstream at all stays `ClassBadRequest` — replaying a genuine shape rejection only replays the rejection. `TestOpaqueBadRequestDoesNotUseAllTargetsDownNotice` (2026-09-24) is repointed at such a body so its no-`all targets down`-notice invariant keeps its coverage, and `TestUpstreamRelayed400RetriesTheWholeLadder` + the two new `TestClassify` cases are the new coverage; the agent test's script is three relays long on purpose, longer than `ClassBadRequest`'s bound, so it fails against the old classification. Verified end to end against a stub gateway replaying the reported body on its first three chat calls: the `origin/main` binary aborts after three attempts with the exact reported line, this branch's binary clears all three and answers (`recovered after the upstream relay`) on the fourth. Shipped defaults, unchanged config. `go build ./...` clean, `gofmt` clean on all three files, `go vet` clean, `go test ./internal/ai/ ./internal/agent/ -count=1` pass; full `go test ./...` keeps only the two pre-existing `cmd/xdev` failures, reproduced on a stashed unmodified tree.*

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/exit-resume-line` — user-reported: "fix the resume with session not print out when exist xdev TUI"). **The `─── resume ───` block on TUI exit printed nothing at all, on every quit.** Not the reachability guard: `runTUI` reads the store for that line through `storeOf()`, and the multi-session tabset (#507) put `tabs.closeAll()` in the *later-registered* exit defer — LIFO order runs the close first, and closeAll empties the slice, so `storeOf()` was already nil when the print defer asked. One capture in the close defer (`exitStore = s`, next to the two `Append`s that already name the current session) is the whole fix; the line then names whatever `/new`, `/fork` and `/resume` last made current, which is the contract the old comment claimed and did not deliver. ponytail: no new state and no reordered defer — the print already needed "the store the close defer flushed", so it reads that value instead of re-deriving it from a set that no longer holds it.*

Verified live, both arms, in a real pty (tmux + `script`, scratch `XDEV_AGENT_DIR` seeded with a real session file, `NO_COLOR` on): `xdev --resume <id>`, one `C-c`, and the block is on the pane with the fixed binary while stock `origin/main` prints a bare `EXITED`. Tests: `TestExitResumeLineSurvivesTheCloseDefer` (the capture-then-`closeAll` order — `storeOf()` really is nil afterwards, and only the captured store still yields the line) and `TestExitLineReadsTheCapturedStore` (the print defer is unreachable without a tty, so it pins the two source facts the fix rests on, the same `os.ReadFile("tui.go")` shape as `TestSendPathsGateOnTheRoom`).

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/mcp-structured-content`, user-reported: "read Earendil's 'You Said No MCP!' post and tell me what I can do to enhance xdev like pi"). **An MCP tool that answered with `structuredContent` beside any text block had that payload silently dropped.** `renderContent` (`internal/mcpclient/mcp.go`) gated the structured channel on an empty body — `if res.StructuredContent != nil && b.Len() == 0` — which was true only in the old SDK world where it was a fallback. SEP-2106 makes it a channel of its own: a server may put a one-line human summary in `content` and the real payload in `structuredContent`, and that payload reached the model as nothing at all. This is the whole of Earendil's "MCP should be much closer to OpenAPI" complaint, and it was one condition. `renderContent` now appends the marshalled `structuredContent` unless the rendered text already carries the identical JSON, so a server that populates both (the Go SDK itself does — `CallToolResult.Content` is "automatically populated with JSON text content corresponding to the structured output value") pays one copy, not two. Everything stays inside the existing 32 KB head+tail sink; no new channel, no new config, no new dependency.

Tests: the E2E fixture server (`internal/mcpclient/testdata/mcpserver`) now answers `echo` with both channels, and `TestConnectListsAndCalls` asserts the payload reaches the registered tool's result — that is a REAL stdio server subprocess driven through the SDK transport, not a synthetic struct. `TestRenderContentKeepsStructuredContentBesideText` covers the unit half, including the both-channels-identical case. Both fail on stock `origin/main` (`structuredContent did not reach the tool result: echo: round-trip`) and pass here; `TestRenderContentIsBounded` (PRD §3.6: bounded everything) and the whole `internal/mcpclient` suite stay green, and `go build ./...`, `go vet`, `./internal/tool ./internal/agent ./internal/ai` are green. `server_status.go` is unformatted on `origin/main` and is untouched here.


*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/tabstrip-deadlock` — user-reported: "xdev keep crashing with tabs and sessions"; root cause read out of the stall dump the watchdog wrote itself). **Clicking a tab's × froze the whole TUI, and the watchdog killed the process 90 seconds later.** `handleTabStripMouse` wrapped its hit lookup in `a.mu.Lock(); defer a.mu.Unlock()` and then called the host's `onTabClose` — and cmd's close path (`closeTabByID`) republishes the open set through `app.SetTabs`, which takes the SAME `sync.Mutex` on the SAME goroutine. Go mutexes are not reentrant, so the loop blocked forever: dump `tui-stall-20261002-151321` (pid 80950) shows `goroutine 1 [sync.Mutex.Lock] → SetTabs ← closeTabByID ← closeTab ← handleTabStripMouse ← handleKey`, with keys, Ctrl+C, Ctrl+D and every repaint unreachable because the loop itself was the thing that was blocked. The label-click path had the same `defer` but survived only by accident: its `onTabPick` already ran on its own goroutine. The rule, stated once: **a host callback fires with `a.mu` released.** The handler now takes the lock only to read `tabHits` and the callback value, then releases before dispatching; `closeTab` documents the same contract. The `session.delete` chord path was already correct (it copies the tab snapshot under the lock, then calls `closeTab` unlocked) — the fix is the mouse path, where the two halves of the handler had drifted apart.*

Of the 131 stall dumps collected on this machine (2026-09-30 → 2026-10-02), this is the only one whose root cause is a lock the App took on itself: the other 130 are long single iterations inside `paint`/a terminal write (the machine idle-throttled, `goroutine 1 [runnable]` in `drawText`/`tcell` `Put`/`Fill`) or an idle `select` in `Run` that the wake-up grace already accounts for. This is the one a code change can fix.

Tests: `TestTabStripCloseClickDoesNotDeadlock` and `TestTabStripLabelClickDoesNotDeadlock` (`internal/tui/tabstrip_deadlock_test.go`) drive the real `handleKey` mouse entry with a host callback that does what cmd's does — `SetTabs` + `AddSystemBlock`, both of which take `a.mu` — and fail by timeout (5s) against the pre-fix `defer`. Verified red/green: reverting `tabstrip.go` alone fails the close test at 5.00s and passes again with the fix restored. Hit coordinates come from the painter's own `tabHits` rectangles, so the tests follow the layout instead of pinning a cell.

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (feat/eval-tool-bridge, #268 — **a cell can now call the harness's tools**): `tools.read({...})` inside an `eval` cell reaches the agent loop and the answer comes back into the cell, so a fan-out of N tool calls costs one assistant message plus what each call did instead of N round trips (pi 1.0 Codemode, dsh `run_code`, opencode `execute`). The route is `docs/decisions/programmatic-tool-calling.md` (§0.1–0.6), settled 2026-10-02 and implemented here: `Kernel.SetRunner` takes a `tool.Runner` — the deferred-tool bridge's own type, so the contract is identical — and `dispatchToolCall` runs the call on its own goroutine, **never under `k.mu`** (§0.3's lock-order finding; `route` returns before the dispatch). Every call lands in `runOneTool`, so plan mode, the approval policy, the interceptor chain and the hooks all still decide, on the inner tool's real name. Three refusals by name rather than counters (§0.5): no runner installed → refuse; `eval` → refuse (a cell that spawns a cell is unbounded recursion); `tool_search`/`tool_describe`/`tool_call` → refuse (`tool.IsBridgeTool`, so a cell cannot reach the registry twice over). The slot is cancellable as §0.3 requires: `reply` claims it with `sync.Once` and `cancelCell`/`markExited`/`stopLocked` all release it, so an interrupted cell raises a Python exception rather than hanging on a host answer that is never coming, and a tool still running sees its context cancelled with the cell (§0.4's "state is lost, don't imply otherwise" holds: kernel state still dies with the process). One new mechanism on the python side and no new dependency: a single stdin reader thread (`_stdin_loop`) answers `tool_result` frames in place and queues cell requests for `main`, and the cell polls its slot so a cell clock or an agent abort becomes a `KeyboardInterrupt` the cell already knows how to report. The seam is wired in `wireAgentMode` beside `WireCatalog` — the third mode seam, and the one most likely to drift, since an unwired kernel refuses every call silently; `TestWireAgentModeWiresEvalKernel` pins it. Tests: `internal/eval/bridge_test.go` (a call round trip, two calls in one kernel, the refusals, the interrupt-does-not-hang case, tool-context cancellation) and the wiring pin. Known: a cell cannot call the model registry from inside — `typesafe`/Jev is reachable as a tool (`tools.typesafe({...})`) but there is no `models.classify` binding, and no `codemode`-only tool axis on the catalog (`tool.Entry` has `deferred` only); both are the next slices, not part of this one. Still open per §0.7: whether a cell's output is one tool result or N lines of what each call did (this PR takes the DSH reading — one result, printed/returned values only).


*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/tty-restore` — user-reported: "xdev sometimes suspends the TTY and crashes the herdr terminal"). **Every terminal restore went through `scr.Fini`, and `scr.Fini` takes the same screen mutex a wedged UI loop is holding.** tcell's `Show` holds `t.Lock` across `draw`, and `draw` ends in the synchronous `syscall.write` to the tty — so a loop wedged in a write to a terminal that stopped draining holds that mutex for the whole episode. Both restore paths then deadlocked on it: the signal guard (`watchTerminalRoutes`) called `fini()` inline before re-raising, so a Ctrl-Z or the kernel's SIGTTOU on a backgrounded launch blocked in the handler and never re-raised — the process left in a raw-mode alt screen with no way out, i.e. the suspended terminal; and the panic guard had the same shape. Evidence on the box: 24 of 134 stall dumps under the data dir name `tcell.(*tScreen).draw → syscall.write` (worst 17m), three live `R+` xdev processes hours old, and the keepalive watchdog logging `duplicate session: fl` while trying to resume a process that was still holding the pty raw. The fix keeps the fast path byte-identical and bounds only the slow one: `restoreTerminalBounded(fini, reason)` (tui_panic.go) gives the tcell-owned restore `restoreGrace` (2s), then restores the settings captured before tcell took the tty — `term.GetState` at `captureTerminal`, released by `releaseTerminal` on the same defer stack as `scr.Fini` — with `term.Restore`, which is an ioctl and cannot block on a write. The alt-screen escape sequence follows, behind `escapeGrace` (500ms) and best effort: the terminal refusing writes is the same one being asked to leave the alt screen, so the settings restore is the guarantee and the escape is the nicety. `golang.org/x/sys` moves from indirect to direct (the test's termios read). Tests: `cmd/xdev/tui_termstate_test.go` — `TestBoundedRestoreKeepsTheFastPath` (a restore that returns in time never reaches the fallback; a nil restore is inert) and `TestTerminalFallbackSurvivesAWedgedWrite`, which runs a child on a real pty under `/usr/bin/script`, never drains its output so the write parks in the kernel, models `scr.Fini` as a restore that never returns, and requires the child to come back with ECHO/ICANON/ISIG restored; it fails against the pre-fix shape with the child exiting 8 (raw tty) and is a hang without the bound. Verified: `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...`, `gofmt` clean on all five touched files, `go test ./internal/tui/` green, `go test ./cmd/xdev/` green except `TestConnectPickerItems` and `TestSkillPromptBlockEmptyWithoutSkills`, which fail identically on a stashed tree (they read the developer's own `~/.xdev` credential store and skill dir, not this change). Deliberate ceiling: a write that is already parked cannot be un-parked — the loop still has to be given up on, which the watchdog does after 90s (#518). This change makes that exit *restore the terminal* instead of inheriting a deadlock, and removes the deadlock from the signal and panic paths entirely. **Update, 2026-10-03 — the upgrade path above shipped.** #537 (merged before this fix) is exactly it: `internal/tui/tty_deadline.go` re-routes tcell's frame write through a second fd on the same terminal with a 250ms deadline, so the loop survives the wedge instead of being restored around. This entry was written against a tree that did not yet have it. The two fixes meet at `cmd/xdev/tui.go` and are independent: #531 makes the restore reachable when the loop is wedged anyway, #537 makes the wedge not happen. A consequence for reading the tests below: `TestTerminalFallbackSurvivesAWedgedWrite` models `scr.Fini` as a restore that never returns, which is the honest unit test of the fallback, but it is not a full reproduction — with #537 in place the loop no longer parks in `syscall.write`, so the deadlock is unreachable by construction on such a tree. Driving the real TUI on a real pty with the reader stopped reproduces the pre-#537 failure and passes after #537; on a post-#537 tree the same run is green with or without #531. So the guard against a regression here is #537's `TestFrameWriteStaysWedgedUntilTheDeadline` plus #531's fast-path test, not a single end-to-end case.

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/tool-arg-coercion` — found while collecting this session's five background subagents: **seven `hub` calls in a row were refused, all of them identically, and the refusals named the wrong field.** The cause is one thing: a model that READS a JSON Schema but is not CONSTRAINED by it sends two spellings the tools' plain `json.Unmarshal` cannot accept, and every tool in the repo decodes its own args by hand.
+
+**(1) A flattened array.** `{"ids":{"item":["hub-1","hub-2"]}}` instead of `{"ids":[...]}`. OpenAI structured outputs serialize a bare `{"type":"array"}` as an object with a single `item` key, and a model trained on that shape emits it even with `strict` off — which is every wire xdev speaks that is not `codex-responses` (`strictTools` is set on `codex_responses.go:31` and nowhere else; the session's model is `opencode/space-bunny-free` on `openai-completions`, i.e. never enforced). The error it produced was `cannot unmarshal object into Go struct field .ids of type []string`. **(2) A quoted scalar.** `{"timeout":"600"}` against `{"type":"number"}` — `cannot unmarshal string into Go struct field .timeout of type float64`. The same batch, 2026-09-23, had `githubArgs.limit` fail the same way as `"20"`. **`tool_describe` was no help**: it prints the JSON Schema, which is exactly the shape the model was getting wrong, and the flat `{"op":...}` shape is not something a deferred-tool bridge can route back.
+
+Both are a wire-level shape fault, not a tool bug, and the fix is one chokepoint rather than 40: `internal/tool/argcoerce.go` `CoerceArgs(schema, args)` walks the tool's OWN declared schema against the payload and repairs only what is unambiguous — a single-key object under a declared array whose one key is one of `item`/`items`/`value`/`values` holding an array unwraps to that array; a string that is exactly an integer/number/boolean for a declared `integer`/`number`/`boolean` becomes that value; a number/bool for a declared `string` becomes its text. It is called at `internal/agent/loop.go:1867`, immediately after the argument bytes are resolved and BEFORE plan mode, the approval policy, the interceptor and the hooks — so every gate judges the arguments the tool will actually receive, and a direct call, a `tool_call` bridge and an `eval` cell all get it from the one site (`runOneTool` is the single entry; `catalog.go:32` and `evalbridge.go:45` both re-enter it). The repair never invents: a two-key wrapper, a non-numeric string for a number, an undeclared key, a non-array under a flatten key, or a non-object payload are all returned byte-identical, so a genuinely wrong call still reaches the tool and still gets the tool's own honest required-argument error. An untouched payload is returned as its ORIGINAL bytes (the decode uses `json.Number`, and a rewrite only re-marshals when `changed`), so nothing that used to work acquires float formatting or key reordering on the way past.
+
+Tests: `internal/tool/argcoerce_test.go` pins both live shapes, the integer-stays-integer rule (`"20"` → `20`, not `20.0`), the nested case (`task`'s `max_turns` inside `tasks[]`), byte-identical passthrough, and five refusal cases. `internal/agent/argcoerce_wire_test.go` `TestRunOneToolRepairsLiveFailureShapes` drives `runOneTool` with the exact arguments that failed seven times and decodes what the tool received — it fails on stock `main` with the field's own error, and it is the pin that the call site is wired. `internal/agent/catalog_test.go` `TestHubToolAcceptsTheLiveFailureShapes` runs the same shape through the REAL `HubTool`, which would otherwise be the tool that fails. Two pre-existing failures in `cmd/xdev` (`TestConnectPickerItems`, `TestSkillPromptBlockEmptyWithoutSkills`) are environment-dependent and reproduce unchanged on `origin/main`.

---

## The in-band tool-argument bug batch (2026-10-02)

Seven `tool_call hub` invocations failed in one turn before any subagent
result came back, and each failure looked like a different bug. The session
transcript (`~/.xdev/agent/sessions/…/2026-10-02T09-18-46.460Z_44bf6c20….jsonl`)
shows they were one: **the model was not wrong about what it wanted, it was
wrong about the JSON envelope, and the tools' decoders refuse anything but the
literal schema.**

```
{"args":{"ids":{"item":["hub-1",…]},"op":"wait","timeout":"600"},"name":"hub"}
```

Two distinct defects in that one payload:

1. **`ids` is an object, not an array.** OpenAI structured outputs serialize a
   bare `{"type":"array"}` as an object with a single `item` key; models
   trained on that shape emit it even on wires with no strict enforcement
   (`openai-completions` has no `strictTools` at all — only `codex_responses`
   does, `internal/ai/codex_responses.go:31`). Every gateway that proxies
   chat/completions inherits this. `hubtool.go:60` declares `IDs []string`, so
   the call is refused before the tool ever sees it.
2. **`timeout` is the string `"600"` for a declared number.** Same root cause
   (the wire does not constrain the model), same refusal.

The reported error compounds the confusion: Go's `encoding/json` names the
*last* field it failed on, so the same call produced
`cannot unmarshal object into … .ids of type []string` when `ids` was the
culprit and `cannot unmarshal string into … .timeout of type float64` when
only `timeout` was wrong. Neither message names the real defect, which is why
the retries wandered (`ids` → `timeout` → `ids:"hub-1"` → dropping both).

This was not new to today: on 2026-09-23 the same envelope shape broke the
`github` tool twice (`cannot unmarshal string into Go struct field
githubArgs.limit of type int` — `"limit":"20"` for an `int`), and on
2026-09-28 it recurred. The batch is old; it was only noticed now because the
`hub` tool has the most array-typed parameters of any tool in the tree.

### Fix: schema-driven coercion at the one chokepoint

`internal/tool/argcoerce.go` walks the tool's declared schema alongside the
argument payload and repairs only what is unambiguous:

| sent | declared | repaired |
|---|---|---|
| `{"item":["a","b"]}` | `array` | `["a","b"]` |
| `"600"` | `number` | `600` |
| `"20"` | `integer` | `20` (never `20.0`) |
| `"true"` | `boolean` | `true` |
| `600` | `string` | `"600"` |

It refuses anything ambiguous — a two-key object, a wrapper whose value is not
an array, a non-numeric string for a number, an undeclared key — so a genuinely
wrong call still reaches the tool and still gets the tool's own honest
required-argument error. A payload that needs no repair keeps its original
bytes (no re-marshal, so a `command` string never acquires float formatting).

It runs at `runOneTool`'s single seam (`internal/agent/loop.go`, just after the
redactor and **before** plan mode, the approval policy, the bash interceptor,
and the hooks), deliberately: those gates must judge the arguments the tool
will actually receive, not the shape the model guessed. One call site covers a
direct call, a `tool_call` bridge (`runCatalogCall`), and an `eval`-kernel cell
(`runEvalTool`) — all three land in `runOneTool`.

Tests: `internal/tool/argcoerce_test.go` (the five shapes, the byte-identical
no-op, and eight ambiguous shapes that must stay untouched) and
`TestHubToolAcceptsTheLiveFailureShapes` in `internal/agent/catalog_test.go`
(the live payload end-to-end through `runOneTool`). Verified red/green: with
the one-line call site reverted the agent test fails with the exact field
error from the field, and passes with it.

*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (fix/tool-arg-coercion — a schema-guided repair of the
two argument-envelope shapes non-strict wires provoke, so a model's typo stops
being seven refusals): the root cause was never the hub tool's schema; it was
that `openai-completions` — the wire this session runs on — sends no strict
enforcement, so a model trained on OpenAI structured outputs flattens a bare
array to `{"item":[…]}` and quotes its numbers, and every tool decoder refuses
both. `tool.CoerceArgs` repairs the unambiguous cases against the tool's own
declared schema and refuses everything else, so the honest error still comes
from the tool.*


*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/tui-frame-deadline`, user-reported: "xdev usually crash the TUI (by suspended) and this causes the crash the herdr terminal pane also"): **the suspended TUI was a frame write that could not return.** tcell flushes a frame with ONE blocking write on the tty (`tScreen.draw` → `buf.WriteTo(t.tty)`), and the pane above xdev can stop draining it — an unfocused pane whose terminal nobody is reading, a resize storm, a suspended child. The pty buffer fills, the write blocks, and the UI loop stops beating: keys, Ctrl+C and output all stop while the process stays alive. Twelve of the 138 dumps in `~/.xdev/agent/dumps` carry that exact stack (`goroutine 1 [syscall]` → `devTty.Write` at `tty_unix.go:53`) with silences from 5s to 17m, and the stall watchdog ended those sessions by restoring the terminal and exiting — a session that died mid-turn, which is what the user reports as "the TUI crashed". The terminal is left raw for the pane too, because Fini is what restores it.

**The fix bounds the write instead of trusting it.** `*os.File` honours a write deadline on a pty slave — measured on this machine with the master never read: an unbounded 4KB write never returns, the same write with a 200ms deadline returns `i/o timeout` — and tcell already discards the error at every flush site, so a timeout IS the drop. `internal/tui/tty_deadline.go` wraps tcell's `Tty` with a second handle on the same terminal (`frameWriteTimeout = 250ms`) and records the drop; `App.draw` then calls `Sync()` once per dropped frame, because tcell marks each cell clean BEFORE the write, so a dropped frame is not self-healing. Net effect: a pane that stopped draining costs one frame, and the session keeps running. `cmd/xdev/tui.go` builds the bounded screen and falls back to `tcell.NewScreen()` when it cannot (no `/dev/tty`, no terminfo); Windows keeps the stock screen, because a console handle has no pty buffer to fill and `SetWriteDeadline` means nothing there (`tty_deadline_windows.go`).

Ceiling, stated deliberately: a deadline, not a second writer goroutine. The upgrade path is a per-frame write queue, which would let a pane paint while its reader lags instead of losing frames; the cost here is that a terminal which cannot keep up loses one frame per 250ms, and each lost frame costs a full repaint, so a wedged reader turns the 30fps tick into ~4fps until it drains.

Tests: `internal/tui/tty_deadline_test.go` — a write to a terminal that cannot drain returns on the deadline and records the drop; `draw()` over that terminal returns instead of blocking, and the dropped frame is repaired in full exactly once; a screen with no deadline flag repaints nothing on every frame. All three fail against the pre-fix shape (deadline removed, repair removed) and pass after. End to end on a real pty with the master never read for 40s while the window is resized every 50ms: stock `origin/main` writes a UI-loop stall dump whose goroutine 1 is blocked in that write path, the fixed binary writes none.
*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (`fix/interceptor-echo` — found by driving the merged #530 in a real print run, not by a test: **the coercion ran, reported `changed=true`, and the tool still received the model's original bytes.**) The unit pins for #530 all passed and the field bug was genuinely fixed at `CoerceArgs` — but the real harness wires an interceptor on every call, and that interceptor ECHOED the unrepaired payload back over the repair. `runOneTool` coerced into the local `args`, then `a.Intercept.ToolCall(ctx, call)` returned `call.Arguments` (still the raw model blob — `hooks.Bus.ToolCall` `internal/hooks/interceptor.go:35` returns `args` unchanged when no hook revises, and `ext.Manager.ToolCall` `internal/ext/ext.go:591` returns `current` unchanged when no extension is subscribed), and the loop's `if len(revised) > 0 { args = revised }` (`loop.go:1939`) overwrote the repair with it. So a call with a hooks bus installed silently reverted to pre-#530 behaviour, while the same call with `Intercept == nil` (every unit test) took the repair. Instrumented print run against `origin/main` @ `8114a58`: `COERCE-STEP … changed=true` immediately followed by `INTERCEPT-REVISED {"ids":["hub-1"],…} -> {"op":"wait","ids":{"item":["hub-1"]},…}` and then the field's own error.
+
+The one-line fix: `loop.go` also writes the repair back onto the call block (`call.Arguments = args`) right after the coercion, so the block the interceptor receives IS the repaired payload, and the echo returns the repaired bytes. A real revision still wins, because the interceptor's return value is applied afterwards exactly as before.
+
+Tests: `TestRunOneToolCoercesPastAnEchoingInterceptor` drives `runOneTool` with an interceptor that behaves like the wired one (returns `call.Arguments`, nil) and the field's exact arguments; it fails on the merged main with `cannot unmarshal object into Go struct field .ids of type []string` and passes with the fix. Verified end to end, not just in a test: a local openai-completions SSE server emitting `{"op":"wait","ids":{"item":["hub-1","hub-2","hub-3"]},"timeout":"1"}` on the wire, driven through the real `xdev print` binary — `hub [ok, 1.025s] wait: nothing settled within the timeout` with the fix, `hub [error, 0s] … malformed arguments` on `8114a58`. `go build ./...`, `go vet ./...` clean; `go test ./...` clean except the two environment-dependent `cmd/xdev` failures that reproduce on `origin/main`.
*Last updated: 2026-10-03 (`fix/resume-status-metrics` — user-reported: "fix xdev total time and token and all metrics in the bottom status bar must be resumed after resume a session"). **Every metric on the status bar was wiped by `/resume` and only the work timer came back.** `App.Reset` zeroes the whole `Status` (↑⇢↓˟, cache-write, cost, rate, TTFT, ctx, tool calls/time, LLM time, turns/steps) — correct as a per-session boundary — but the replay path then re-banked only what it could measure cheaply: `SetContextReplay`, `SetWork`, `SetLLMTime`, `SetSessionCounts`. The token buckets and the spend were never re-banked, so a session reopened at "0s" with no tokens and no cost for work it had already done and been charged for (`internal/tui/app.go` `Reset`, `cmd/xdev/tui.go` `workOf`/`ttftOf`/`countsOf`). Fix: one more re-base on the same replayed path — `usageOf` (`cmd/xdev/tui.go`) sums the persisted per-message `ai.Usage` the way the live hook banks it (`AddUsage`/`AddCost`, `tuiHooks.OnEvent`), and `App.SetSessionUsage` (`internal/tui/app.go`) REPLACES the buckets + cost, the same no-double-count contract `SetWork`/`SetSessionCounts` keep. Deliberately NOT re-based: `CtxUsed` (live occupancy — `SetContextReplay` measures it) and `Rate` (a history cannot re-measure the last decoded message's speed). The adoption path is ONE function, `replaySession(app, msgs)` (`cmd/xdev/tui.go`), shared by every site that rebuilds a history — startup `--continue`/`--resume`, `focusTab` (tab switching), `branchReplay` (tree navigation/branch), and everything routed through `swapStoreTo` (fork, import, picker resume). A metric wired at two of three sites and missed at the third is the defect itself, so the copies are gone rather than patched. Tests: `cmd/xdev/sessionops_test.go` (`TestUsageOfSumsPersistedBuckets` — every bucket off the persisted split, no-usage and pre-usage messages contributing zero) and `cmd/xdev/tui_metric_wire_test.go` (`TestResumedSessionKeepsStatusMetrics` — Reset then the real replay path, reading the PAINTED status row: 68.5k tokens, 98% cache hit, 6s work, and `$0.0133` in /usage). Pre-existing failures on `main`, unrelated: `TestConnectPickerItems` (reads this machine's cursor credential state) and `TestSkillPromptBlockEmptyWithoutSkills` (reads this machine's installed skills).

*Last updated: 2026-10-03 (`fix/resume-work-timer` — the residue #535 left): **a resumed session's work timer read at about half of what it showed before it closed.** `workOf` (`cmd/xdev/tui.go`) summed only assistant `DurationMS`, so the re-based active-work total excluded every tool span — while the LIVE timer counts them, because `markRun` banks the whole run span (thinking, streaming and tools alike). Measured on a real 1576-assistant-message session file: assistant spans 7,728s, tool spans 7,512s over 1682 calls, so the resumed figure was 49.3% low and `/usage`'s "tool time · N% of active time" line divided two different denominators (it was even suppressed whenever the tool share exceeded 100%). `workOf` now counts `ai.RoleToolResult` spans too, and the tool spans were already persisted — `toolResultMsg` (`internal/agent/loop.go`) stamps `DurationMS` from OnToolStart→end for `/trajectory` and resume; only the sum ignored them. `TestWorkOfSumsAssistantSpans` becomes `TestWorkOfSumsRunSpans` (15.5s, tool span included), and the resume wire test now carries a tool result so the painted row must read `15s`. Two ceilings are stated in the code rather than faked: a bang-mode (`!bash`) call never reaches the store, so its span is still lost across a resume; and same-batch tool calls run CONCURRENTLY (`MaxToolWorkers` = 6), so summing spans can exceed the wall time they took — the live timer measures the run's wall clock and cannot over-count. Removing the second needs run spans persisted as an entry type of their own, which the store does not have.
*Last updated: 2026-10-02 (Claude Code's diff + syntax palette, branch `feat/cc-diff-colors`): a file change now reads the way it reads in CC. Two things changed together, and the second is what makes the first safe. (1) **The diff's claim moved from coloured text to a band.** The +/- marker wears the palette's own green/red — the pair the status line already paints a clean/dirty tree in, so one system owns both — and the rest of the row is body ink on a flat band, with the changed words inside a -/+ pair on a stronger one. The bands are CC's own tints (`internal/theme`: `tool_diff_added_bg` #022800 / `tool_diff_added_word_bg` #044700 and the red equivalents on groknight; the near-white pair on grokday). This **reverses the 2026-09-15 "red on red" fix**, deliberately and on the reasoning it got wrong: that one painted the row's own WORDS from a fixed RGB and laid a band tinted from the same ink underneath, so the words drowned in their own colour. Here the ink paints one marker cell and each band is a tint of its own rather than of the marker's ink, so there is no ink for a band to bury. The four band slots are xdev's own vocabulary, NOT part of omp's 66-token contract, so an imported omp theme loads unchanged and simply paints no band (marker inks only — its previous look). (2) **`Builtins` no longer marks the diff slots terminal-default**, so the built-ins own the inks; `TestBuiltinThemesLeaveDiffToTerminal` became `TestBuiltinThemesPaintTheDiff` (owns the inks, bands differ from each other, added ≠ removed). The dock's "+N/−M" counts now take the MARKER ink (`ds.add.mark`), since the panel has no band behind a two-character count, and the diff overlay repaints each row from its own runs instead of re-inking every +/- row in one colour — which had flattened the band and the word emphasis back into plain text. **Color-blind mode tints the bands too** (`internal/theme/colorblind.go`): a green band under a blue marker would leave the red/green pair standing in the one place it is hardest to read, so each band is a mix of the pair ink toward the canvas (86%/74% dark, 85%/72% light) rather than a table of eight hexes. **Fenced-code tokens moved to CC's scope map** (v2.1.287 hljs emitter, One Dark / One Light): keyword `#f92672`, string `#e6db74`, number `#be84ff`, type/function `#a6e22e`, comment `#75715e` on groknight — 9 lines × 2 palettes, no renderer change. **`diffStyle` reads `NO_COLOR` itself**, for the same reason `codeStyleFor` does: tcell drops colour at emission, so a run that picked an ink would still look painted in a cell dump while the terminal shows one ink. Under NO_COLOR the diff falls back to attributes only — the terminal's own text ink, bold on the changed words, no band — which is the pre-band look rather than a colourless shell. **Verified in a real TTY, not just a cell dump**: a tmux pane with the binary rebuilt on this branch, driving `xdev --resume` over a hand-seeded session whose tool result carries a `unifiedDiff`, then reading `capture-pane -e` for the SGR the terminal actually got — `48;2;2;40;0` / `48;2;61;1;0` (row bands), `48;2;4;71;0` / `48;2;92;2;0` (word bands), and the palette's own `38;2;158;206;106` / `38;2;247;118;142` markers. That live run is also what caught the first version's one real defect: the band was on the words but NOT on the marker cell or the pad, so the marker was a hole in its own stripe. `bandRow` now puts the row background on every run including the marker, and `wordStyle` takes the row style so a word band replaces the row band instead of stacking on it.
